Introduction and Qualifications 
1

3

4 
 
5 
Q. 
By whom are you employed and in what capacity? 
6 
A. 
I am employed by National Grid USA Service Company, Inc., a subsidiary 
7 
of National Grid USA (“National Grid”), as the Manager of Gas Load 
8 
Forecasting and Analysis.  I am responsible for supervising the 
9 
development of the gas load forecasts for National Grid’s gas utilities, 
10 
including The Brooklyn Union Gas Company d/b/a National Grid NY 
11 
(“KEDNY”) and KeySpan Gas East Corporation d/b/a National Grid 
12 
(“KEDLI”) (collectively, the “Companies”).

14 
Q.  
Please describe your educational background and professional 
15 
experience. 
16 
A.

17 
Bachelor of Science in Geology.  From 1981 to 1989, I worked as a 
18 
Research Associate with Jensen Associates, Inc. of Boston where I was 
19 
responsible for developing a variety of computer forecasting models to

analyze natural gas supply and demand for interstate pipeline and local 
1 
distribution companies.  Upon accepting employment with Boston Gas

3 
natural gas resource requirements of the company’s customers and 
4 
managing its resource-planning process.  In 1998 and 1999, I undertook the 
5 
same responsibility for Essex Gas Company and Colonial Gas Company, 
6 
respectively.  In 2000, I assumed responsibility for modeling and 
7 
forecasting the natural gas resource requirements of KEDNY and KEDLI.

III. 
Overview of the Companies’ Retail Gas Deliveries 
7 
Q. 
Please provide an overview of the Companies’ historic gas deliveries 
8 
from 2013 through 2017. 
9 
A. 
For KEDNY, actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 1.484 billion 
10 
therms to 1.677 billion therms to all customers, excluding non-core (SC 
11 
18-5A Non-Core Transport) and electric generation (SC 18 Small Electric 
12 
Generator Transport and SC 20 Large Electric Generator Transport) 
13 
(Exhibit ___ (TEP-1A)).  “Retail” refers to gas delivered and metered at 
14 
the customers’ burner tips, while “wholesale” refers to gas received and 
15 
metered flowing into the Companies’ distribution systems.  The range in 
16 
volumes of actual retail gas deliveries was primarily driven by fluctuations 
17 
in the weather, such as the colder-than-normal calendar year 2014 and the 
18 
warmer-than-normal calendar years of 2016 and 2017.  On a weather-
19 
normalized basis, KEDNY’s retail gas deliveries to all customers, 
20 


--- Page 10 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
8 of 44 
 
 
excluding non-core and electric generation, have grown at a compound 
1 
annual growth rate of 2.3 percent per annum from 1.480 billion therms in 
2 
calendar year 2013 to 1.624 billion therms in calendar year 2017 (Exhibit 
3 
___ (TEP-8A)).  This growth reflects recovery from the 2007-2009 
4 
recession and the decrease in domestic natural gas prices. Historic actual 
5 
billed sales by month and by class for 2013-2017 are provided in Exhibit 
6 
___ (TEP-7A) for KEDNY.  Historic weather-normalized billed sales by 
7 
month and by class for 2013-2017 are provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-8A) 
8 
for KEDNY. 
9 
 
10 
For KEDLI, actual retail gas deliveries have ranged from 0.912 billion 
11 
therms to 1.013 billion therms to all customers, excluding small and large 
12 
electric generation (SC 7 Small Electric Generator Transport and SC 14 
13 
Large Electric Generator Transport) (Exhibit ___ (TEP-1B)).  Similar to 
14 
KEDNY, the range in volumes of actual retail gas deliveries for KEDLI 
15 
was primarily driven by weather fluctuations.  On a weather-normalized 
16 
basis, KEDLI’s retail gas deliveries to all customers excluding non-core 
17 
and electric generation have grown at a compound annual growth rate of 
18 
3.3 percent per annum from 0.879 billion therms in calendar year 2013 to 
19 
1.000 billion therms in calendar year 2017 (Exhibit ___ (TEP-8B)).

Similar to KEDNY, this growth reflects recovery from the 2007-2009

IV. 
Gas Delivery Forecast 
3 
Q. 
Please describe KEDNY’s individual service and revenue class 
4 
distinctions. 
5 
A. 
KEDNY’s gas tariff (PSC No. 12) includes the following active service 
6 
classifications: SC 1, SC 2, SC 3, SC 4, SC 6, SC 7, SC 17, SC 18, SC 20, 
7 
and SC 21.  Certain service classifications contain distinct revenue classes. 
8 
The revenue classes include Residential Non-Heat, Residential Heat, 
9 
Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial Heat, and Industrial.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe KEDLI’s individual service and revenue class 
12 
distinctions. 
13 
A.  
KEDLI’s gas tariff (PSC No. 1) includes the following active service 
14 
classifications: SC 1, SC 2, SC 3, SC 4, SC 5, SC 7, SC 9, SC 12, SC 13, 
15 
SC 14, SC 15, SC 16, and SC 17.  Certain service classifications contain 
16 
distinct revenue classes.  The revenue classes include Residential Non-
17 
Heat, Residential Heat, Commercial Non-Heat, Commercial Heat, and 
18 
Industrial.

20 
 
21

Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
11 of 44 
 
 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the delivery forecasts for KEDNY 
1 
and KEDLI. 
2 
A. 
On an annual basis, the Companies prepare their gas load forecasts based 
3 
on econometric/statistical forecast models developed for residential, 
4 
commercial/industrial, and multifamily rate groups.  The instant forecasts 
5 
were based on historical billing data from March 2007 through February 
6 
2018.   
7 
 
8 
Although the Companies maintain their customer data at the internal rate 
9 
code level (67 unique rate codes for KEDNY and 52 unique rate codes for 
10 
KEDLI), the data from these rate codes were aggregated into six rate 
11 
groups: Residential Heating, Residential Non-Heating, Commercial, 
12 
Multi-Family, Temperature-Controlled, and Other.  By aggregating its 
13 
data into these six groups, the Companies developed population sets that 
14 
were large enough for statistical modeling as well as free of variations that 
15 
are not driven by economic factors.  The tables below summarize the 
16 
service classifications that comprised the rate groups: 
17 
 
18 
KEDNY Rate Groups 
Rate Group 
Service Classifications 
Residential Heating 
SC 1B and SC 17-1B 
Residential Non-Heating
SC 1A and SC 17-1A 


--- Page 14 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
12 of 44 
 
 
Commercial 
SC 2-1, SC 2-2, SC 17-2-1, and 
SC 17-2-2
Multi-Family 
SC 3, SC 17-3, and SC 18-6M 
Temperature-Controlled
SC 6 and SC 18
Other 
Other
 
1 
KEDLI Rate Groups 
Rate Group 
Service Classifications 
Residential Heating 
SC 1B and SC 5-1B
Residential Non-Heating
SC 1A and SC 5-1A
Commercial 
SC 2A, SC 2B, SC 5-2A, and SC 
5-2B
Multi-Family 
SC 3 and SC 5-3
Temperature-Controlled
SC 12 and SC 13
Other 
Other
 
2 
Two different models were developed for each rate group: (i) a model to 
3 
forecast the number of customers (the number of active, flowing meters), 
4 
and (ii) a model to forecast use-per-customer.  The Companies’ volume 
5 
forecasts are then the product of number of customers multiplied by the 
6 
use-per-customer.  The customer count and use-per-customer models are 
7 
developed using linear regression techniques, and are selected based on 
8 
their adjusted R squared after accounting for the effects of autocorrelation 
9 
and heteroscedasticity.   
10 
 
11 
In the number of customer (meter count) models, the independent 
12 
variables are a combination of time trends and economic variables, such 
13 
as population, households, employment, and gas and oil prices.  In the 
14 


--- Page 15 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
13 of 44 
 
 
models calculating use-per-customer, the independent variables are a 
1 
combination of time trends, heating degree days, and economic variables, 
2 
such as population, housing stock, income, employment, unemployment, 
3 
gross domestic product, and gas and oil prices.   
4 
 
5 
KEDNY and KEDLI obtained the historic data and forecasts of the 
6 
independent variables from a leading economic research and forecasting 
7 
firm as well as energy prices from the U.S. Department of Energy, Energy 
8 
Information Administration, and used this data to develop the econometric 
9 
models and generate load forecasts.   
10 
 
11 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the customer count forecasts. 
12 
A. 
Because the Companies’ economic data is available at the quarterly level, 
13 
the Companies developed their models at the quarterly level, as well.  The 
14 
Companies obtained the historic and projected economic and demographic 
15 
data used as the independent variables in the regression equations from 
16 
Moody’s economy.com (publication date: February 2018).  The 
17 
Companies obtained energy price data from the U.S. Department of 
18 
Energy, Energy Information Administration (“DOE/EIA”).   
19 
 
20 


--- Page 16 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
14 of 44 
 
 
For its meter count (customer count) models, the Companies aggregated 
1 
their monthly meter count data, which is available by internal rate code to 
2 
the level of the six rate groups.  The Companies then took the meter count 
3 
at the end of each February as quarter one, the end of May as quarter two, 
4 
the end of August as quarter three, and the end of November as quarter 
5 
four. 
6 
 
7 
Linear regression analyses were then performed on the six rate groups for 
8 
each company to develop its meter count models.  After completing the 
9 
meter count modeling, the results were then allocated to the monthly level 
10 
using spline functions and to the internal rate code level based on most 
11 
recent ratios of the various internal rate codes to their rate group. 
12 
 
13 
Summary results of the KEDNY models can be found in Exhibit ___(TEP-
14 
10A).  Summary results of the KEDLI models can be found in Exhibit 
15 
___(TEP-10C).  The meter count models for each rate group and the data 
16 
used to derive them are described in more detail below.   
17 
 
 
18 


--- Page 17 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
15 of 44 
 
 
Q. 
Please describe how you derived the use-per-customer forecasts. 
1 
A. 
The historic monthly use-per-customer values were obtained by dividing 
2 
the total billed therms for each month by the number of billed customers 
3 
for the month.   
4 
 
5 
Because the Companies’ economic data is available at the quarterly level, 
6 
the Companies developed their models at the quarterly level, as well.  The 
7 
Companies obtained the historic and projected economic and demographic 
8 
data used as the independent variables in the regression equations from 
9 
Moody’s economy.com (publication date: February 2018).  The 
10 
Companies obtained energy price data from the DOE/EIA and weather 
11 
data from the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration 
12 
(“NOAA”) and National Weather Service (“NWS”).   
13 
 
14 
For its use-per-customer models, each company aggregated its monthly 
15 
volume data, which is available by internal rate code to the level of its six 
16 
rate groups and to four quarters per year (the end of each February as 
17 
quarter one, the end of May as quarter two, the end of August as quarter 
18 
three, and the end of November as quarter four).  To calculate the quarterly 
19 
use-per-customer, the Companies then divided the quarterly volumes by 
20 
the corresponding meter counts at the end of each quarter. 
21 


--- Page 18 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
16 of 44 
 
 
Linear regression analyses were then performed on the six rate groups for 
1 
each company to develop its use-per-customer models.  After completing 
2 
its use-per-customer modeling, quarterly volumes were calculated from 
3 
the product of quarterly meter count times quarterly use-per-customer.  
4 
The volume results were then allocated to the internal rate code level based 
5 
on most recent ratios of the various internal rate codes to their rate group 
6 
and to the monthly level based on the historical average monthly-to-
7 
quarterly percentages. 
8 
 
9 
Summary results of the KEDNY models can be found in Exhibit ___(TEP-
10 
10B).  Summary results of the KEDLI models can be found in Exhibit 
11 
___(TEP-10D).  The use-per-customer models for each rate group and the 
12 
data used to derive them are described in more detail below.   
13 
 
14 
Q. 
How is the weather variable in the models determined? 
15 
A. 
Weather is a fundamental variable in modeling the natural gas 
16 
requirements of the Companies’ customers.  The Companies used gas day 
17 
(10am – 9am) heating degree days (“HDD”) based on temperature 
18 
observations from the NWS Central Park, New York City, New York 
19 
weather station in the models for both KEDNY and KEDLI.  For the 
20 
forecasts, as shown in Exhibit ___ (TEP-11), the Companies defined their 
21 


--- Page 19 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
17 of 44 
 
 
normal HDD based on a thirty-year average of the period from July 1988 
1 
to June 2018 as 4,535 HDD. 
2 
 
3 
Q. 
Please explain how the initial forecasts were adjusted for initiatives in 
4 
the Companies’ service territories that could impact the amount of 
5 
natural gas consumed over the next five years. 
6 
A. 
The Companies’ volume forecasts are based on models of meter count and 
7 
use-per-customer developed for each residential, commercial/industrial, 
8 
and multifamily rate class.  The dependent use-per-customer variable in 
9 
the Companies’ load forecast models is the quotient of historic sales data 
10 
divided by historic meter count.  The Companies’ historic sales data 
11 
includes the impact of actual energy efficiency savings from both the New 
12 
York State Energy Research and Development Authority (“NYSERDA”) 
13 
and KEDNY/KEDLI sponsored energy efficiency programs.  The 
14 
forecasts include this trend in continuing load reduction based on the 
15 
historic successes in energy efficiency reductions in load.  No further 
16 
adjustments were made to the forecasts to reflect additional energy 
17 
efficiency reductions. 
18 
 
19 
As discussed in the direct testimony of the Gas Infrastructure and 
20 
Operations Panels and Company Witness Elizabeth Arangio, the 
21 


--- Page 20 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
18 of 44 
 
 
Companies have signed a precedent agreement with an upstream pipeline 
1 
project, known as the Northeast Supply Enhancement project (“NESE”), 
2 
that will provide access to additional gas supplies necessary to relieve 
3 
supply constraints in the Companies’ service territory.  If NESE does not 
4 
become available by the 2020/21 winter season, the Companies will not 
5 
be able to prudently add new or additional gas load without jeopardizing 
6 
the Companies’ ability to provide safe, reliable service to its existing firm 
7 
customers.  The Companies’ forecasts assume that the NESE project will 
8 
be approved and in service, and that no moratoriums will be placed on the 
9 
establishment of new gas services in the Companies’ territories during this 
10 
time period.  The Companies’ will update their forecasts in Corrections 
11 
and Updates if the NESE project is not approved. 
12 
 
13 
Q. 
Has the forecast methodology that you described been used previously 
14 
by the Companies? 
15 
A. 
Yes.  Econometric forecasting has been used by KEDLI and KEDNY for 
16 
business, gas resource, and infrastructure planning for years, and was used 
17 
in the Companies’ previous rate cases (Cases 16-G-0058 and 16-G-0059).  
18 
The only change to the Companies’ process is to focus on producing retail 
19

20 
which reflects discussions with Department of Public Service Staff in the 
21

previous Niagara Mohawk Power Corporation rate case (Case 17-G-

2

3

Q. 
Please discuss the models used by the Companies to forecast sales and 
13 
customer counts. 
14 
A. 
As I explained previously, the Companies’ load growth forecasts are based 
15 
on econometric/statistical forecast models developed for each residential, 
16 
commercial, multifamily, temperature-controlled, and other rate groups 
17 
and that two different models were developed for each class: (i) a model 
18 
to forecast the number of customers (meter count) and (ii) a model to 
19 
forecast use-per-customer.   
20 
 
21 


--- Page 25 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
23 of 44 
 
 
For the meter count models, the Companies modeled the entire time series 
1 
of data available for each model.  The candidate independent variables are 
2 
time trends, population, households, employment, and gas and oil prices.   
3 
 
4 
For the use-per-customer models, the Companies divided the time series 
5 
data for each rate group into two categories – peak season and off-peak 
6 
season – to reflect periods during which heating customers are using the 
7 
heating equipment and not using their heating equipment.  The use-per-
8 
customer during each of those time periods would be different.  The 
9 
breakpoint chosen by the Companies was 600 HDD for the quarter.  The 
10 
candidate independent variables are time trends, heating degree days, 
11 
population, housing stock, income, employment, unemployment, gross 
12 
domestic product, and gas and oil prices.    
13 
 
14 
The details of the statistical modeling are provided in Exhibit ___ (TEP-
15 
10A) (KEDNY meter count), Exhibit ___ (TEP-10B) (KEDNY use-per-
16 
customer), Exhibit ___ (TEP-10C) (KEDLI meter count), and Exhibit ___ 
17 
(TEP-10D) (KEDLI use-per-customer). 
18 
 
 
19 


--- Page 26 ---
Testimony of Theodore Poe, Jr. 
24 of 44

4

5

6 
variable was most appropriate as the data reflects a market not driven by 
7 
economic factors, but a constrained market where the non-heating 
8 
customers are converted to heating customers at a fixed rate and new 
9 
customers are added to the system at a fixed rate.
